From lighter to darker blue, plot of the boundary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(ct)^{\nicefrac {1}{\alpha }}$$\end{document}(ct)1α for the powers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\nicefrac {k}{3}$$\end{document}α=k3 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=1,2,3,4,5$$\end{document}k=1,2,3,4,5. This shows how the region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{|x|\le (ct)^{\nicefrac {1}{\alpha }}\}$$\end{document}{|x|≤(ct)1α} shrinks as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}α→0 (equiv. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}p→∞)

Organization of the Paper

In Sect. 2 we review the literature and compare it with the present work. In Sect. 3 we give a heuristic explanation of Theorem 1.5. In Sect. 4 we write the relaxed Euler equation (23) for radially symmetric self-similar subsolutions. In Sect. 5 we derive the conditions under which an admissible subsolution exists, and we construct an example. Then, we prove Theorems 1.1 & 1.5. Finally, we analyze the borderline case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow 2$$\end{document}α→2 in Sect. 6.

Brief Background

In this section we review the literature on non-uniqueness for the Euler equation and compare it with the present work.

Self-similarity and Symmetry Breakdown

The global existence of solutions was extended to the vorticity class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1\cap L^p$$\end{document}L1∩Lp for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1<p\le \infty $$\end{document}1<p≤∞ by DiPerna and Majda [29] (see also [56, Chapter 10]). In contrast to Yudovich well-posedness Theorem, uniqueness is not expected to hold for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p<\infty $$\end{document}p<∞. Roughly speaking, the Sobolev embedding implies continuity of the velocity field, and thus existence of the Lagrangian map X by the Peano Theorem, but the modulus of continuity does not satisfy the Osgood uniqueness criterion. Let us formulate this question as a conjecture, which remains open to the best of our knowledge.

Conjucture 2.1

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2<p<\infty $$\end{document}2<p<∞ there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ \in L^2$$\end{document}v∘∈L2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^\circ \in L^1\cap L^p$$\end{document}ω∘∈L1∩Lp, with the property that there is more than one weak solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C_t L^2$$\end{document}v∈CtL2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in L_t^\infty (L^1\cap L^p)$$\end{document}ω∈Lt∞(L1∩Lp) to the Euler equation (4).

In the recent groundbreaking work [71, 72] Vishik solved Conjecture 2.1 for the forced Euler equation (see also the book [2]) 18a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t\omega +\textrm{div}(v\omega )&=f, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tω+div(vω)=f,18b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v&=\nabla ^\perp \Delta ^{-1}\omega , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v=∇⊥Δ-1ω,18c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega |_{t=0}&=\omega ^\circ . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ω|t=0=ω∘.

Theorem 2.1

(Vishik’s non-uniqueness Theorem) For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2<p<\infty $$\end{document}2<p<∞ there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ \in L^2$$\end{document}v∘∈L2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^\circ \in L^1\cap L^p$$\end{document}ω∘∈L1∩Lp and a force \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f=\textrm{rot}g\in L_t^1(L^1\cap L^p)$$\end{document}f=rotg∈Lt1(L1∩Lp) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in L_t^1\,L^2$$\end{document}g∈Lt1L2, with the property that there are infinitely many weak solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C_t L^2$$\end{document}v∈CtL2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in L_t^\infty (L^1\cap L^p)$$\end{document}ω∈Lt∞(L1∩Lp) to the forced Euler equation (18).

Remarkably, Albritton, Brué, and Colombo [1] proved recently non-uniqueness of Leray solutions for the forced 3D Navier-Stokes equation by adapting properly Vishik’s construction into the cross section of an axisymmetric vortex ring. This approach is framed within the program of Jia, Šverák, and Guillod [46, 53] on the conjectural non-uniqueness of Leray solutions of the (unforced) 3D Navier–Stokes equation.

In the recent investigation [5, 6] Bressan, Murray, and Shen showed numerical evidence toward the validity of Conjecture 2.1. Their work is also based on self-similarity and symmetry breakdown. Their approach suggests two different ways of regularizing a non-radially symmetric version of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _s$$\end{document}ωs, leading to either one or two algebraic spirals. In contrast to Vishik’s spectral analysis, their construction relies on a smart system of adapted coordinates due to Elling [33, 34] (see also the recent work of García and Gómez-Serrano for gSQG [39]).

Relation Between Theorem 1.1 and Vishik’s Non-uniqueness Theorem

Theorem 1.1 has certain connections with Vishik’s non-uniqueness Theorem. Firstly, both theorems share the same initial data (12). Secondly, Vishik’s construction is also based on finding a suitable self-similar velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{v}}$$\end{document}v¯. Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{v}}$$\end{document}v¯ is a modification of the power-law vortex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta |x|^{-\alpha }x^\perp $$\end{document}β|x|-αx⊥ in a disc of radius \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2t^{\nicefrac {1}{\alpha }}$$\end{document}2t1α, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β is a sufficiently large constant. Thirdly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{v}}$$\end{document}v¯ is also truncated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi $$\end{document}χ to guarantee integrability at infinity.

In spite of these similarities, both the results and the proofs differ significantly. Concerning the results, Vishik’s non-uniqueness Theorem solves Conjecture 2.1 in the natural vorticity class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1\cap L^p$$\end{document}L1∩Lp by introducing a force, while Theorem 1.1 shows non-uniqueness without forcing by considering distributional vorticities. Our original motivation was indeed to explore the possibility of removing the force in Theorem 2.1 by means of convex integration, but paying the price of the low-regularity inherent to these constructions. The first obvious attempt was to absorb Vishik’s force into the Reynolds stress. However, it was not immediate for us that the corresponding subsolution was admissible. More precisely, we first needed to derive the conditions under which a radially symmetric self-similar subsolution yields admissible solutions via convex integration. After this, instead of checking if Vishik’s vortex satisfies these conditions, it becomes easier to construct our own profile H.

Convex Integration

As we mentioned, in this work we deal with a weaker version of Conjecture 2.1: the integrability condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in L_t^\infty (L^1\cap L^p)$$\end{document}ω∈Lt∞(L1∩Lp) is removed (for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0) and then necessarily (4) is replaced by (1). The first result in this direction is due to Scheffer [65]: there exist Euler velocities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in L_{t,x}^2$$\end{document}v∈Lt,x2 with compact support in space-time. Latter, this construction was simplified by Shnirelman [66]. In the seminal work [25] De Lellis and Székelyhidi proved the same result in the energy space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_t^\infty L^2$$\end{document}Lt∞L2, and for any space dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\ge 2$$\end{document}d≥2.

Observe that these solutions show non-uniqueness for the trivial initial datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ =0$$\end{document}v∘=0. Non-uniqueness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_t^\infty L^2$$\end{document}Lt∞L2 was generalized by Wiedemann [73] for every divergence-free \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ \in L^2$$\end{document}v∘∈L2 (see [54] for recent improvements of the regularity). For smooth initial data, the aforementioned solutions necessarily increase the energy, as a consequence of the weak–strong uniqueness principle. In this sense, a divergence-free \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ \in L^2$$\end{document}v∘∈L2 is called wild if it admits infinitely many admissible solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in L_t^\infty L^2$$\end{document}v∈Lt∞L2.

In [26] De Lellis and Székelyhidi initiated the investigation on non-uniqueness of admissible solutions, upon which this work is based. Recall that the property “admissible” includes both conservative (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=E(0)$$\end{document}E=E(0)) and dissipative (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E<E(0)$$\end{document}E<E(0)) solutions. In his famous work [64] Onsager conjectured, in the context of the zeroth law of turbulence, the threshold regularity for the validity of the energy conservation of weak solutions to the Euler equation (in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^3$$\end{document}T3). Onsager’s conjecture, which is nowadays a theorem, can be stated as follows: Any weak solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C_t C^\gamma $$\end{document}v∈CtCγ to the Euler equation with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma >\nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}γ>13 conserves the energy.For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\gamma <\nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}0<γ<13 there exist weak solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C_t C^\gamma $$\end{document}v∈CtCγ to the Euler equation which do not conserve the energy.Part ((a)) was fully proved by Constantin, E, and Titi [17], after a partial result of Eyink [35]. Part (b) was solved more recently by Isett [50], and by Buckmaster, De Lellis, Székelyhidi, and Vicol [9]. The last achievement took a decade of refinements of the convex integration method, and the study of its connection with turbulent flows is still an active research area (see e.g. the recent works [44, 63]). Recently, the dissipative part of the 2D Onsager conjecture was solved by Giri and Radu [45]. The convex integration method has been applied to other 2D active scalar equations (see e.g. [22, 52, 67]) as well as the transport equation (see e.g. [59–61]).

Any weak solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C_t C^\gamma $$\end{document}v∈CtCγ to the Euler equation with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma >\nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}γ>13 conserves the energy.

Any weak solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C_t C^\gamma $$\end{document}v∈CtCγ to the Euler equation with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma >\nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}γ>13 conserves the energy.

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\gamma <\nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}0<γ<13 there exist weak solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C_t C^\gamma $$\end{document}v∈CtCγ to the Euler equation which do not conserve the energy.

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\gamma <\nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}0<γ<13 there exist weak solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C_t C^\gamma $$\end{document}v∈CtCγ to the Euler equation which do not conserve the energy.

Unfortunately, it seems not possible with the current convex integration techniques to construct solutions, neither with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in C^{\gamma }$$\end{document}v∈Cγ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nicefrac {1}{3}\le \gamma <1$$\end{document}13≤γ<1, nor with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in L^p$$\end{document}ω∈Lp for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\ge 1$$\end{document}p≥1 in two dimensions. In [7] Brué and Colombo constructed a Cauchy sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _k$$\end{document}ωk in the Lorentz space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{1,\infty }$$\end{document}L1,∞, whose velocities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_k$$\end{document}vk converge to an anomalous weak solution v. In [8] Buck and Modena adapted the previous construction for the Hardy space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^p$$\end{document}Hp for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nicefrac {2}{3}<p<1$$\end{document}23<p<1. This space is also weaker than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1$$\end{document}L1 but, in contrast to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{1,\infty }$$\end{document}L1,∞, it already embeds into the space of distributions.

Relation Between Theorem 1.3 and Onsager’s Conjecture

Coming back to the initial value problem, Theorem 1.3 gives presumably the first example of wild data with Hölder regularity above the Onsager critical exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nicefrac {1}{3}\le \gamma <1$$\end{document}13≤γ<1 (see [10] for a convex integration construction in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\nicefrac {1}{2}-}$$\end{document}C12- for the forced 3D Euler equation). Below the Onsager critical exponent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\gamma <\nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}0<γ<13, Daneri, Runa, and Székelyhidi [23] proved that the set of wild data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ \in C^{\gamma }$$\end{document}v∘∈Cγ is a dense subset of the divergence-free vector fields in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 (see also [28]). However, to the best of our knowledge, it was not known neither concrete examples of wild data in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^\gamma $$\end{document}Cγ, nor even the size of their set of singular points (see [27] for estimates of the singular set of times). In this regard, Theorem 1.3 provides an explicit example with a minimal singular set: a single point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{0\}$$\end{document}{0}. We remark that, although the aforementioned literature on the Onsager conjecture (b) is posed on the 3D periodic domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^3$$\end{document}T3, our solutions can be trivially adjust to this setting. However, in this case the singular set becomes a line \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{0\}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}{0}×T. On this matter, it would be interesting to explore if there might be other 3D wild data with smaller singular sets.

Remarkably, Székelyhidi [68] provided the first concrete example of a wild datum for the unstable vortex sheet \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ (x)=\textrm{sgn}(x_2)$$\end{document}v∘(x)=sgn(x2), which corresponds to the limiting case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\rightarrow 1$$\end{document}p→1. This initiated a promising program on modeling hydrodynamical instabilities via convex integration: see [58, 68] for the Kelvin-Helmholtz, [40, 42, 43] for the Rayleigh-Taylor, and [11–13, 19, 37, 48, 57, 62, 69] for the Saffman-Taylor instabilities.

Yudovich Spaces

In [77] Yudovich extended his uniqueness result for unbounded vorticities for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \omega _s\Vert _{L^p}$$\end{document}‖ωs‖Lp has moderate growth as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}p→∞. Let us recall Taniuchi’s (non-localized) version [70] of this generalization (see also [14]): Given a non-decreasing function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta :[1,\infty )\rightarrow [1,\infty )$$\end{document}Θ:[1,∞)→[1,∞), a vorticity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω belongs to the Yudovich space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^\Theta $$\end{document}YΘ if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \omega \Vert _{Y^\Theta }:=\sup _{p\in [1,\infty )}\frac{\Vert \omega \Vert _{L^p}}{\Theta (p)}<\infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ω‖YΘ:=supp∈[1,∞)‖ω‖LpΘ(p)<∞.Then, the Euler equation (4) is globally well-posed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^\Theta $$\end{document}YΘ if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta $$\end{document}Θ satisfies the Osgood type condition19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _3^\infty \frac{\,\textrm{d}p}{p\Theta (p)}=\infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫3∞dppΘ(p)=∞.Notice that Theorem 1.4 corresponds to the particular case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^1=L^1\cap L^\infty $$\end{document}Y1=L1∩L∞. In this regard, it would be interesting to explore if Theorem 1.1 could be extended to Yudovich spaces for which (19) fails.

Global existence has been proved in other vorticity classes (see e.g. the recent work of Crippa and Stefani [21] and the references therein). In [70] Taniuchi extended it to the Yudovich space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^\Theta $$\end{document}YΘ for which the Osgood condition (19) is weakened by20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _3^\infty \frac{\,\textrm{d}p}{p\Theta (\log p)}=\infty . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫3∞dppΘ(logp)=∞.In this regard, Conjecture 2.1 could be also stated for Yudovich spaces satisfying the existence condition (20) but not the uniqueness condition (19).

Energy Conservation/Dissipation

In this section we discuss the energy conservation/dissipation of weak solutions to the Euler equation in terms of the vorticity. For simplicity of presentation we consider the periodic domain. The same results hold in the euclidean space mutatis mutandis.

We start by recalling that Onsager’s conjecture (a) is a corollary of the following Besov type criterion (see e.g. [15, 30]): Any weak solution v to the Euler equation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Td satisfying21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{|y|\rightarrow 0}\int _0^T\int _{\mathbb {T}^d}\frac{|v(t,x+y)-v(t,x)|^3}{|y|}\,\textrm{d}x\,\textrm{d}t = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}lim|y|→0∫0T∫Td|v(t,x+y)-v(t,x)|3|y|dxdt=0,conserves the energy. Although this condition is independent of the dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\ge 2$$\end{document}d≥2, it has stronger implications in the 2D case. As a first easy consequence, by the Sobolev embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{1,p}\subset C^{1-\nicefrac {2}{p}}$$\end{document}W1,p⊂C1-2p and (a), it follows that the energy is conserved for any weak solution with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^p$$\end{document}Lp control on vorticity for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p>3$$\end{document}p>3. However, the energy conservation can be extended for smaller p’s by taking more advantage of (21). Let us recall the argument from [30, Proposition 6]. Firstly, by applying the Hölder inequality to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _y v=v(x+y)-v(x)$$\end{document}δyv=v(x+y)-v(x), we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \delta _y v\Vert _{L^3} \le \Vert \delta _y v\Vert _{L^p}^\theta \Vert \delta _y v\Vert _{L^q}^{1-\theta } \quad \text {with}\quad \frac{1}{3}=\frac{\theta }{p}+\frac{1-\theta }{q}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖δyv‖L3≤‖δyv‖Lpθ‖δyv‖Lq1-θwith13=θp+1-θq.Secondly, by using the Sobolev embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{1,p}\subset L^{\frac{2p}{2-p}}$$\end{document}W1,p⊂L2p2-p and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \delta _y v\Vert _{L^p}\le |y|\Vert v\Vert _{W^{1,p}}$$\end{document}‖δyv‖Lp≤|y|‖v‖W1,p, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \delta _y v\Vert _{L^3}\le C|y|^\theta \Vert v\Vert _{W^{1,p}} \quad \text {with}\quad \theta =\frac{5}{3}-\frac{2}{p}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖δyv‖L3≤C|y|θ‖v‖W1,pwithθ=53-2p.Therefore, the energy conservation criterion (21) is satisfied for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta >\nicefrac {1}{3}$$\end{document}θ>13, or equivalently \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p>\nicefrac {3}{2}$$\end{document}p>32. This result was extended to the borderline case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=\nicefrac {3}{2}$$\end{document}p=32 by Cheskidov, Lopes Filho, Nussenzveig Lopes, and Shvydkoy [16]. Furthermore, they constructed a velocity field with vorticity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{\nicefrac {3}{2}-}$$\end{document}L32- exhibiting non-vanishing energy flux. This suggests that there might exist dissipative solutions of the 2D Euler equation with vorticity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{\nicefrac {3}{2}-}$$\end{document}L32-. This threshold is somehow related to our construction: the energy dissipation rate of our subsolution vanishes at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=0$$\end{document}t=0 if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha <\nicefrac {4}{3}$$\end{document}α<43, where recall \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^\circ =\omega _s\in L^{\nicefrac {2}{\alpha }-}$$\end{document}ω∘=ωs∈L2α-. Let us give a heuristic explanation of this phenomenon.

Heuristic Explanation of Theorem 1.5

First of all, it is convenient to rewrite the Euler equation (1) as 22a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t v+\textrm{div}(v\ocircle v)+\nabla q&= 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tv+div(v⊚v)+∇q=0,22b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{div}v&=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}divv=0,22c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v|_{t=0}&=v^\circ , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v|t=0=v∘, where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v\ocircle v:=v\otimes v-\frac{1}{2}|v|^2I_2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v⊚v:=v⊗v-12|v|2I2,is the projection of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\otimes v$$\end{document}v⊗v into the space of symmetric and traceless matrices, and the trace has been absorbed by the Bernoulli pressure\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q:=p+\frac{1}{2}|v|^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}q:=p+12|v|2.Let us analyze the non-uniqueness/energy-dissipation scenario in the Euler equation. Firstly, we introduce some notation. Concerning non-uniqueness: Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ \in L^2$$\end{document}v∘∈L2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{div}v^\circ =0$$\end{document}divv∘=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{rot}v^\circ \in L^1\cap L^p$$\end{document}rotv∘∈L1∩Lp, let us denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=S(v^\circ )$$\end{document}S=S(v∘) by the space of admissible solutions to the Euler equation, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_p=S_p(v^\circ )$$\end{document}Sp=Sp(v∘) by the subset of S formed by velocities with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in L_t^\infty (L^1\cap L^p)$$\end{document}ω∈Lt∞(L1∩Lp). Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_p$$\end{document}Sp is non-empty by [29]. With this notation, we can rewrite Yudovich’s well-posedness Theorem: if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=\infty $$\end{document}p=∞, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=S_\infty =\{v\}$$\end{document}S=S∞={v} where v is the Yudovich solution. Similarly, we can reformulate Conjecture 2.1: for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2<p<\infty $$\end{document}2<p<∞ there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^\circ $$\end{document}v∘ satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|S_p(v^\circ )|>1$$\end{document}|Sp(v∘)|>1, and Theorem 1.1: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|S(v^\circ )|=\infty $$\end{document}|S(v∘)|=∞ for the initial data (12). Concerning energy dissipation: Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in S$$\end{document}v∈S we denote its energy by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E:=\int _{\mathbb {T}^2} e\,\textrm{d}x, \quad \quad e:=\frac{1}{2}|v|^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E:=∫T2edx,e:=12|v|2,and its dissipation by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D:=E(0)-E. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}D:=E(0)-E.Next, we give a condition for non-uniqueness/energy-dissipation in terms of averaged solutions. Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ a probability measure on S, we define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{v}}:=\int _{S}v\,\textrm{d}\mu , \quad \quad {\bar{\sigma }}:=\int _{S}(v\ocircle v)\,\textrm{d}\mu , \quad \quad {\bar{q}}:=\int _{S}q\,\textrm{d}\mu . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v¯:=∫Svdμ,σ¯:=∫S(v⊚v)dμ,q¯:=∫Sqdμ.The triple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) satisfies the relaxed Euler equation 23a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t{\bar{v}}+\textrm{div}{\bar{\sigma }}+\nabla {\bar{q}}&= 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tv¯+divσ¯+∇q¯=0,23b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{div}{\bar{v}}&=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}divv¯=0,23c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{v}}|_{t=0}&=v^\circ . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v¯|t=0=v∘. The (relaxed) energy was obtained by De Lellis and Székelyhidi in [26, Lemma 3]24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{E}}=\int _{\mathbb {T}^2}{\bar{e}}\,\textrm{d}x, \quad \quad {\bar{e}}:=\frac{1}{2}|{\bar{v}}|^2 +\lambda _{\max }({\bar{v}}\ocircle {\bar{v}}-{\bar{\sigma }}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E¯=∫T2e¯dx,e¯:=12|v¯|2+λmax(v¯⊚v¯-σ¯),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{\max }$$\end{document}λmax denotes the largest eigenvalue. The crucial facts are that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{e}}$$\end{document}e¯ is convex and agrees with e on S. By applying the Jensen inequality and the Fubini-Tonelli Theorem, we get 25a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{E}}&=\int _{\mathbb {T}^2}{\bar{e}}({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }})\,\textrm{d}x =\int _{\mathbb {T}^2}{\bar{e}}\left( \int _{S}(v,v\ocircle v)\,\textrm{d}\mu \right) \textrm{d}x \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E¯=∫T2e¯(v¯,σ¯)dx=∫T2e¯∫S(v,v⊚v)dμdx25b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\le \int _{\mathbb {T}^2}\int _{S}{\bar{e}}(v,v\ocircle v)\,\textrm{d}\mu \,\textrm{d}x =\int _S\int _{\mathbb {T}^2}e(v)\,\textrm{d}x\,\textrm{d}\mu \nonumber \\&=E(0)-\int _S D(v)\,\textrm{d}\mu . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}≤∫T2∫Se¯(v,v⊚v)dμdx=∫S∫T2e(v)dxdμ=E(0)-∫SD(v)dμ. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{E}}\le E(0)$$\end{document}E¯≤E(0). The last inequality is strict in two situations: If the Jensen inequality (25a) is strict. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{e}}$$\end{document}e¯ is strictly convex on (the convex envelope) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{spt}(\mu )$$\end{document}spt(μ). In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textrm{spt}(\mu )|>1$$\end{document}|spt(μ)|>1.If the dissipative term in (25b) is non-vanishing. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D(v)>0$$\end{document}D(v)>0 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in S$$\end{document}v∈S.In short, (1) concerns non-uniqueness and (2) concerns energy dissipation. On the one hand, (1) is to be expected in the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1<p<\infty $$\end{document}1<p<∞. On the other hand, (2) cannot occur if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{spt}(\mu )\subset S_p$$\end{document}spt(μ)⊂Sp in the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nicefrac {3}{2}\le p<\infty $$\end{document}32≤p<∞, while it might be possible if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1<p<\nicefrac {3}{2}$$\end{document}1<p<32.

If the Jensen inequality (25a) is strict. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{e}}$$\end{document}e¯ is strictly convex on (the convex envelope) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{spt}(\mu )$$\end{document}spt(μ). In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textrm{spt}(\mu )|>1$$\end{document}|spt(μ)|>1.

If the Jensen inequality (25a) is strict. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{e}}$$\end{document}e¯ is strictly convex on (the convex envelope) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{spt}(\mu )$$\end{document}spt(μ). In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\textrm{spt}(\mu )|>1$$\end{document}|spt(μ)|>1.

If the dissipative term in (25b) is non-vanishing. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D(v)>0$$\end{document}D(v)>0 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in S$$\end{document}v∈S.

If the dissipative term in (25b) is non-vanishing. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D(v)>0$$\end{document}D(v)>0 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ-a.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in S$$\end{document}v∈S.

We have seen that the existence of a solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) to (23) with strictly decreasing energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{E}}$$\end{document}E¯ is linked to either non-uniqueness or energy dissipation. Our third main result Theorem 1.5 shows the existence of this solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯), which is called a subsolution in the convex integration framework, for the initial data (12). Remarkably, the initial energy dissipation of the subsolution is imperceptible (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t{\bar{E}}|_{t=0}=0$$\end{document}∂tE¯|t=0=0) in the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nicefrac {3}{2}\le p<\infty $$\end{document}32≤p<∞. Below \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=\nicefrac {3}{2}$$\end{document}p=32, the energy dissipation rate is more abrupt, which might reflect the contribution of the dissipation (2) to the Jensen gap (1).

By virtue of the h-principle for the Euler equation discovered by De Lellis and Székelyhidi [26] Theorem 1.5 implies the existence of infinitely many admissible solutions, and thus Theorem 1.1 follows as corollaries. Furthermore, the flexibility of the h-principle allows to prescribe any energy functional e strictly greater than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{e}}$$\end{document}e¯ on the region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\{|x|\le (ct)^{\nicefrac {1}{\alpha }}\}$$\end{document}Ω={|x|≤(ct)1α}. Thus, it is possible to select e making the energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E=\int e\,\textrm{d}x$$\end{document}E=∫edx either constant (conservative) or decreasing (dissipative). Here we do not consider increasing energies (non-admissible).

Other Admissibility Criteria

In spite of these non-uniqueness results, there exist other admissibility criteria that rule out some of these solutions. The first criterion is to belong to the natural space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_p$$\end{document}Sp. In the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nicefrac {3}{2}\le p<\infty $$\end{document}32≤p<∞, any weak solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in S$$\end{document}v∈S with decreasing energy cannot belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_p$$\end{document}Sp because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_p\subset S_{\text {con}}:=S\cap \{E=E(0)\}$$\end{document}Sp⊂Scon:=S∩{E=E(0)}. In contrast, by taking E decreasing in the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1<p<\nicefrac {3}{2}$$\end{document}1<p<32, Theorem 1.1 shows presumably the first example of dissipative solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in S$$\end{document}v∈S with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^p$$\end{document}Lp vorticity data, although this does not necessarily imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_p\nsubseteq S_{\text {con}}$$\end{document}Sp⊈Scon. The second criterion is the vanishing viscosity limit. It was shown in [16, Theorem 2] that any physically realizable solution conserves the energy for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1<p\le \infty $$\end{document}1<p≤∞. This scaling gap in the energy conservation between ideal solutions and ideal limits has been observed in other equations of Hydrodynamics (see [18] for SQG and [36] for MHD). By taking E constant in the regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1<p<\infty $$\end{document}1<p<∞, Theorem 1.1 shows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|S_{\text {con}}|=\infty $$\end{document}|Scon|=∞ for the initial data (12), although this does not necessarily implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|S_p|>1$$\end{document}|Sp|>1. For radially symmetric solutions, a third way to rule out non-uniqueness could be the stability of 2D viscous vortices (see e.g. [38]). In such a case, the proof of non-uniqueness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^p$$\end{document}Lp physical solutions would require less symmetric initial data. Another prerequisite satisfied by physicial solutions is the local energy (in)equality (see e.g. [26, 30]). Globally dissipative solutions have been constructed in the last years via convex integration (see e.g. [24, 41, 44, 51]). We do not explore this property here.

H-Principle for Symmetric Subsolutions

In this section we write the h-principle for the Euler equation of De Lellis and Székelyhidi [26] for 2D radially symmetric self-similar subsolutions. We start by recalling the definitions of weak solution and subsolution to the Euler equation.

Definition 4.1

A triple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})\in C_t(L^2\times L^1\times L^1)$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯)∈Ct(L2×L1×L1) where26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} {\bar{\sigma }}_2 \\ {\bar{\sigma }}_2 &{} -{\bar{\sigma }}_1 \end{array}\right] ,\quad \quad {\bar{q}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v¯=v¯1v¯2,σ¯=σ¯1σ¯2σ¯2-σ¯1,q¯,is a subsolution to the Euler equation (22) if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{v}}$$\end{document}v¯ is weakly divergence-free and27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^T\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}({\bar{v}}\cdot \partial _t\Phi +{\bar{\sigma }}:\nabla \Phi + {\bar{q}}\textrm{div}\Phi )\,\textrm{d}x\,\textrm{d}t=-\int _{\mathbb {R}^2}v^\circ \cdot \Phi |_{t=0}\,\textrm{d}x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0T∫R2(v¯·∂tΦ+σ¯:∇Φ+q¯divΦ)dxdt=-∫R2v∘·Φ|t=0dx,holds for every test function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \in C_c^1([0,T)\times \mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}Φ∈Cc1([0,T)×R2). The pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,q¯) is a weak solution to the Euler equation if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{v}}\ocircle {\bar{v}},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,v¯⊚v¯,q¯) is a subsolution.

Next, we recall the h-principle for the Euler equation [26, Proposition 2]. Recall the definition of the energy functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{e}}$$\end{document}e¯ (24).

Theorem 4.1

(H-principle for the Euler equation) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω be a non-empty open subset of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,T]\times \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}(0,T]×R2 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in C(\Omega )$$\end{document}e∈C(Ω) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e{\textbf{1}}_{\Omega }\in C_tL^1$$\end{document}e1Ω∈CtL1. \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) to the Euler equation satisfying the following properties:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{\bar{\sigma }}\ne {\bar{v}}\ocircle {\bar{v}}\} \subseteq \Omega $$\end{document}{σ¯≠v¯⊚v¯}⊆Ω.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯) maps continuously \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{\bar{e}}<e\}$$\end{document}{e¯<e}.Then, there exist infinitely many weak solutions (v, q) to the Euler equation with Bernoulli’s pressure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯) maps continuously \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{{\bar{e}}<e\}$$\end{document}{e¯<e}.
