By virtue of Theorem 4.1, the proof of non-uniqueness of admissible solutions to the Euler equation is reduced to find a subsolution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) with non-empty \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω and strictly decreasing energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{E}}=\int {\bar{e}}\,\textrm{d}x$$\end{document}E¯=∫e¯dx. In this case, we will say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) is an admissible subsolution.

Complex Coordinates

In this section we rewrite the relaxed Euler equation (23) in complex coordinates\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x=x_1+ix_2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x=x1+ix2.In this setting, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z,w\in \mathbb {R}^2\simeq \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}z,w∈R2≃C we denote as usual\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |z|=\sqrt{z_1^2+z_2^2}, \quad \quad z^*=z_1-iz_2, \quad \quad z^\perp =iz=-z_2+iz_1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|z|=z12+z22,z∗=z1-iz2,z⊥=iz=-z2+iz1,and also\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} z\cdot w&=(zw^*)_1=z_1w_1+z_2w_2,\\ z\cdot w^\perp&=(zw^*)_2=z_2w_1-z_1w_2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}z·w=(zw∗)1=z1w1+z2w2,z·w⊥=(zw∗)2=z2w1-z1w2.By slight abuse of the notation, we identify\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla =\partial _1+i\partial _2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇=∂1+i∂2,and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) in (26) with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{v}}={\bar{v}}_1+i{\bar{v}}_2, \quad \quad {\bar{\sigma }}={\bar{\sigma }}_1 + i{\bar{\sigma }}_2, \quad \quad {\bar{q}}={\bar{q}}+i0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v¯=v¯1+iv¯2,σ¯=σ¯1+iσ¯2,q¯=q¯+i0.

Proposition 4.1

The relaxed Euler equation (23) is written in complex coordinates as 28a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t{\bar{v}}+\nabla ^*{\bar{\sigma }}+\nabla {\bar{q}}&= 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tv¯+∇∗σ¯+∇q¯=0,28b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla \cdot {\bar{v}}&=0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇·v¯=0,28c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{v}}|_{t=0}&=v^\circ , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v¯|t=0=v∘, and the energy (24) equals29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{e}}=\frac{1}{2}|{\bar{v}}|^2+\left| \frac{1}{2}{\bar{v}}^2-{\bar{\sigma }}\right| . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e¯=12|v¯|2+12v¯2-σ¯.Furthermore, the subsolution is a solution to the Euler equation if and only if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{\sigma }}=\frac{1}{2}{\bar{v}}^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σ¯=12v¯2.

Proof

On the one hand,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{div} \left[ \begin{array}{rr} {\bar{\sigma }}_1 &{} {\bar{\sigma }}_2 \\ {\bar{\sigma }}_2 &{} -{\bar{\sigma }}_1 \end{array}\right]&=\left[ \begin{array}{c} \partial _1{\bar{\sigma }}_1 + \partial _2{\bar{\sigma }}_2 \\ \partial _1{\bar{\sigma }}_2 - Radial Symmetry

In this section we write the relaxed Euler equation (28) in polar coordinates\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x=re^{i\theta }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x=reiθ,for radially symmetric subsolutions. More precisely, we assume that the fluid is rotating around the origin: the velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{v}}$$\end{document}v¯ is of the form30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{v}}(t,x):=h(t,r)ie^{i\theta }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v¯(t,x):=h(t,r)ieiθ,for some real-valued h, to be determined. Under this choice, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{v}}$$\end{document}v¯ is automatically divergence-free (see (35)) and the vorticity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{\omega }}=\textrm{rot}{\bar{v}}$$\end{document}ω¯=rotv¯ is radially symmetric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{\omega }}(t,x)=g(t,r)$$\end{document}ω¯(t,x)=g(t,r), where h and g are related by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} rg=\partial _r(rh). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}rg=∂r(rh).In order to compare \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{\sigma }}$$\end{document}σ¯ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}{\bar{v}}^2=-\frac{1}{2}h^2e^{2i\theta }$$\end{document}12v¯2=-12h2e2iθ, it seems convenient to take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{\sigma }}$$\end{document}σ¯ of the form31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{\sigma }}(t,x):=-w(t,r)e^{2i\theta }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}σ¯(t,x):=-w(t,r)e2iθ,for some complex-valued w, to be determined. Finally, we also assume (although it can be deduced from the equation) that the Bernoulli pressure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{q}}$$\end{document}q¯ is radially symmetric, and then (by slight abuse of the notation) we write32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{q}}(t,x):=q(t,r), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}q¯(t,x):=q(t,r),for some (real-valued) q, to be determined.

Corollary 4.1

The energy (34) is minimized in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_1$$\end{document}w1 by taking38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} w_1:=\frac{1}{2}h^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}w1:=12h2.Under the choice (38), the relaxed Euler equation (33) is written as 39a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _r q&=gh, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂rq=gh,39b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _r(r^2w_2)&=r^2\partial _ th, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂r(r2w2)=r2∂th,39c\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} h|_{t=0}&=h^\circ , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h|t=0=h∘, and the energy (34) equals40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\bar{e}}=\frac{1}{2}h^2+|w_2|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e¯=12h2+|w2|.Furthermore, the subsolution is a solution to the Euler equation if and only if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Proof

It follows from (34), (36) and (37). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

By virtue of Corollary 4.1, the functions w, q and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{e}}$$\end{document}e¯ are determined by h. Therefore, the construction of an admissible subsolution is reduced to find a profile h with non-vanishing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_2$$\end{document}w2 and strictly decreasing energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{E}}=\int {\bar{e}}\,\textrm{d}x$$\end{document}E¯=∫e¯dx.

Scaling Symmetry

In this section we write the relaxed Euler equation (39) for self-similar subsolutions. The (relaxed) Euler equation possess a two-parameter scaling symmetry (see e.g. [2]): If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) is a (sub)solution and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ,\mu >0$$\end{document}λ,μ>0, then41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{} & {} {\bar{v}}_{\lambda ,\mu }(t,x) =\frac{\lambda }{\mu }{\bar{v}}(\lambda t,\mu x), \quad {\bar{\sigma }}_{\lambda ,\mu }(t,x)\nonumber \\{} & {} =\Big (\frac{\lambda }{\mu }\Big )^2{\bar{\sigma }}(\lambda t,\mu x), \quad {\bar{q}}_{\lambda ,\mu }(t,x) =\Big (\frac{\lambda }{\mu }\Big )^2{\bar{q}}(\lambda t,\mu x), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v¯λ,μ(t,x)=λμv¯(λt,μx),σ¯λ,μ(t,x)=(λμ)2σ¯(λt,μx),q¯λ,μ(t,x)=(λμ)2q¯(λt,μx),define another (sub)solution. This corresponds to the physical dimensions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}{}[x]=L, \quad \quad [t]=T, \quad \quad [{\bar{v}}]=\frac{L}{T}, \quad \quad [{\bar{\sigma }}]=[{\bar{q}}]=\Big (\frac{L}{T}\Big )^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[x]=L,[t]=T,[v¯]=LT,[σ¯]=[q¯]=(LT)2.We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) is self-similar if it is invariant under the scaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\alpha \sim T$$\end{document}Lα∼T for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha >0$$\end{document}α>0, that is, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})_{\lambda ,\mu }=({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯)λ,μ=(v¯,σ¯,q¯) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ,\mu >0$$\end{document}λ,μ>0 given by the relation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda =\nicefrac {1}{t}=c\mu ^{\alpha }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ=1t=cμα,for some parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,c>0$$\end{document}α,c>0.

We assume that the triple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) given by (30)–(32) and (38) is self-similar for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,c>0$$\end{document}α,c>0. In this section we prove Theorems 1.1 & 1.5 by constructing first admissible subsolutions, and then invoking the h-principle for the 2D Euler equation.

Lemma 5.1

(1st condition for H) Under the choice (47), the support of the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_2$$\end{document}W2 to (43b) is contained in [0, 1] if and only if H satisfies48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (4-\alpha )\int _0^1\xi ^2H\,\textrm{d}\xi =1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(4-α)∫01ξ2Hdξ=1.

Proof

Proposition 5.1

Suppose (47)(48) hold. Then, the solution to the relaxed Euler equation (43) is given by50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Q(\xi ):=\left\{ \begin{array}{cl} \displaystyle q_s(1)-\int _{\xi }^{1}GH\,\textrm{d}\zeta , &{} 0<\xi \le 1,\\[0.4cm] q_s(\xi ), &{} \xi >1, \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Q(ξ):=qs(1)-∫ξ1GHdζ,0<ξ≤1,[0.4cm]qs(ξ),ξ>1,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_s$$\end{document}qs as in (46), and51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W_2(\xi ):=\left\{ \begin{array}{cl} \displaystyle \xi H-\frac{4-\alpha }{\xi ^2}\int _{0}^{\xi }\zeta ^2H\,\textrm{d}\zeta , &{} 0<\xi \le 1,\\[0.4cm] 0, &{} \xi >1. \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W2(ξ):=ξH-4-αξ2∫0ξζ2Hdζ,0<ξ≤1,[0.4cm]0,ξ>1.As a result, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})=(v_s,v_s\ocircle v_s,q_s)$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯)=(vs,vs⊚vs,qs) outside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{|x|\le (ct)^{\nicefrac {1}{\alpha }}\}$$\end{document}{|x|≤(ct)1α}.

Proof

The initial condition (43c) is automatically satisfied by (47). The other two equations (43a)(43b) can be integrated (recall (49)). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The Energy

In this section we compute the energy dissipation rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \partial _t{\bar{e}}\,\textrm{d}x$$\end{document}∫∂te¯dx, and derive the conditions under which it becomes negative. Recall that the energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{e}}$$\end{document}e¯ is determined by H through the choices (30)–(32), (38), (42), (47) and (48).

Proposition 5.2

The Growth Rate c

In this section we select c maximizing the energy dissipation rate.

Proposition 5.3

Suppose (54) holds. Then, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \partial _t{\bar{e}}\,\textrm{d}x<0$$\end{document}∫∂te¯dx<0 if and only if55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0<c<\frac{\alpha }{2(2-\alpha )}\frac{A}{B}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0<c<α2(2-α)AB.Furthermore, the energy dissipation rate is maximized56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {R}^2}\partial _t{\bar{e}}\,\textrm{d}x =-2\pi \left( \frac{A}{4-\alpha }\right) ^{\frac{4-\alpha }{\alpha }} \left( \frac{\alpha }{B}\right) ^{\frac{2(2-\alpha )}{\alpha }} t^{\frac{4-3\alpha }{\alpha }}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫R2∂te¯dx=-2πA4-α4-αααB2(2-α)αt4-3αα,by taking57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c:=\frac{\alpha }{4-\alpha }\frac{A}{B}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}c:=α4-αAB.

Proof

The first statement (55) follows immediately from (52). For (56), we need to maximize the functional\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(c)=\frac{2\pi }{\alpha }c^{\frac{2(2-\alpha )}{\alpha }} \left( A-\frac{2(2-\alpha )}{\alpha }Bc\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(c)=2παc2(2-α)αA-2(2-α)αBc.Since F is concave with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F'(c)=\frac{2\pi }{\alpha } \frac{2(2-\alpha )}{\alpha } c^{\frac{4-3\alpha }{\alpha }} \left( A-\frac{4-\alpha }{\alpha }Bc\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F′(c)=2πα2(2-α)αc4-3ααA-4-ααBc,it follows that F attains its maximum at (57) with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F(c)=2\pi \left( \frac{A}{4-\alpha }\right) ^{\frac{4-\alpha }{\alpha }} \left( \frac{\alpha }{B}\right) ^{\frac{2(2-\alpha )}{\alpha }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(c)=2πA4-α4-αααB2(2-α)α.This concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proposition 5.4

We finish this section by computing the energy dissipation rate for the particular ansatz H given in (58). Similarly, it should be possible to construct profiles H with better regularity.

Proposition 5.5

Let H be the profile (58) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=0$$\end{document}b=0. Proof

The Truncation

In this section we prove Theorems 1.1 & 1.5. Let us fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\alpha <2$$\end{document}0<α<2. Recall that the inconvenience of considering the self-similar subsolutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({\bar{v}},{\bar{\sigma }},{\bar{q}})$$\end{document}(v¯,σ¯,q¯) from the previous sections is that they have infinite energy. This is because their tails are not integrable. The h-principle for the Euler equation [25] can be easily modified to construct velocities in this class. As a result, we show non-uniqueness of dissipative solutions to the Euler equation for the (truncated) point vortex datum.
